[Israel](9282173.md) and the [Palestinians](23267.md) are engaged in an ongoing military and political conflict about [land](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Territory) and [self-determination](29269.md) within the former territory of [Mandatory Palestine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mandatory_Palestine).[^cite_note-40][^cite_note-41][^cite_note-bbc2-42] Key aspects of the conflict have included [Palestinian refugees](74681.md), the [Israeli occupation of the West Bank](59076253.md) and [Gaza Strip](76465951.md), the [status of Jerusalem](4024423.md), [Israeli settlements](15123.md), borders, security, water rights,[^cite_note-43] the [permit regime in the West Bank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israeli_permit_regime_in_the_West_Bank) and [in the Gaza Strip](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israeli_permit_regime_in_the_Gaza_Strip), [Palestinian freedom of movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_freedom_of_movement),[^cite_note-WorldBankReport-44] and the [Palestinian right of return](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_right_of_return).

The conflict has its origins in the rise of [Zionism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zionism) in the late 19th century in Europe, a movement which aimed to establish a Jewish state through [colonization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colonization) in the [region of Palestine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Region_of_Palestine).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKhalidi20207–8-45][^cite_note-46] The Zionist movement garnered the support of an imperial power in the 1917 [Balfour Declaration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balfour_Declaration) issued by Britain, which promised to support the creation of a "[Jewish homeland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homeland_for_the_Jewish_people)" in Palestine. Following [British occupation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Occupied_Enemy_Territory_Administration) of the formerly [Ottoman](22278.md) region during [World War I](4764461.md), Mandatory Palestine was established as a [British mandate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mandate_for_Palestine). Increasing [Jewish immigration](351513.md) led to the rapid growth of the [Yishuv](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yishuv), or Jewish population in Palestine, heightening [tensions and conflict](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intercommunal_conflict_in_Mandatory_Palestine) between Jews and Arabs over land and economic issues.[^cite_note-47][^cite_note-48] The 1936 [Arab general strike](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_general_strike) led to the [Peel Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peel_Commission) investigation, and began the [1936–1939 Arab revolt in Palestine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1936%E2%80%931939_Arab_revolt_in_Palestine), which demanded an end to British colonial rule and its support for Zionism.[^cite_note-49][^cite_note-Rashid_Khalidi4-50] Eventually, the inability of British government to resolve tensions led to the creation of the [United Nations Special Committee on Palestine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Special_Committee_on_Palestine). The [United Nations](31769.md) later voted to adopt their recommendations as a [partition plan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Partition_Plan_for_Palestine) in 1947. The plan was rejected by the [Arab Higher Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Higher_Committee) and the [Arab League](52625.md),[^cite_note-51] triggering the [1947–1948 civil war in Mandatory Palestine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1947%E2%80%931948_civil_war_in_Mandatory_Palestine).

During the ensuing [1948 Palestine war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1948_Palestine_war), the State of Israel [was founded](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israeli_Declaration_of_Independence) and more than half of the mandate's predominantly Palestinian Arab population [fled or were expelled](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1948_Palestinian_expulsion_and_flight) by Israeli forces, referred to by Palestinians as the [Nakba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nakba). By the [end](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1949_Armistice_Agreement) of the war, Israel was established on most of the former mandate's territory, and the [Gaza Strip](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Administration_of_the_Gaza_Strip_by_Egypt) and the [West Bank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Administration_of_the_West_Bank_by_Jordan) were controlled by [Egypt](8087628.md) and [Jordan](7515964.md) respectively.[^cite_note-Ilan_Pappe3-52][^cite_note-Benny_Morris3-53] Since the 1967 [Six-Day War](29328.md), Israel has been occupying the West Bank and the Gaza Strip, known collectively as the [Palestinian territories](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_territories). Two Palestinian uprisings against Israel and its occupation erupted in 1987 and 2000, the [first](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_intifada) and [second intifadas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_intifada) respectively. Israel's occupation resulted in Israel constructing [illegal settlements](25408080.md) there, creating a system of institutionalized discrimination against Palestinians under its occupation referred to as [Israeli apartheid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israeli_apartheid).[^cite_note-israpa-54] This discrimination includes Israel's denial of Palestinian refugees from their right of return and [right to their lost properties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israeli_land_and_property_laws#Absentees'_Property_Laws). Israel has also [drawn international condemnation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_United_Nations_resolutions_concerning_Israel) for [violating the human rights of the Palestinians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_rights_violations_against_Palestinians_by_Israel).[^cite_note-55]

The international community, with exceptions including the [United States](3434750.md) and Israel that prefer bilateral negotiations, has largely[^cite_note-56][^cite_note-57] been in consensus since the 1980s regarding a settlement of the conflict on the basis of a [two-state solution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Two-state_solution) along the [1967 borders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Green_Line_\(Israel\)) and a just resolution for Palestinian refugees. In recent years, public support for a two-state solution has decreased, with Israeli policy reflecting an interest in maintaining the occupation, rather than seeking a permanent resolution to the conflict. In 2007, Israel tightened its [blockade of the Gaza Strip](19224310.md) and made official its policy of isolating it from the West Bank. Since then, Israel has framed its relationship with Gaza in terms of the [laws of war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laws_of_war), rather than in terms of its status as an [occupying power](816214.md). In [a July 2024 advisory opinion](72240449.md), the [International Court of Justice](14918.md) (ICJ) determined that Israel continues to illegally occupy the West Bank and Gaza Strip.

Since 2006, Israel has fought [several wars](16346266.md) with the ruling party in the Gaza Strip, [Hamas](13913.md). Attacks by [Hamas-led armed groups in October 2023](74998317.md) in Israel, which resulted in nearly 1,200 deaths, were followed by [another war](74998519.md), which has caused widespread destruction, loss of life, [mass population displacement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gaza_Strip_evacuations), a [humanitarian crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gaza_humanitarian_crisis_\(2023%E2%80%93present\)), and a [famine](75828824.md) in the Gaza Strip.[^cite_note-Dannenbaum_Dill_2024-58][^cite_note-IPC_22_August_2025-59] Israel's actions in Gaza during this war [have been described](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_humanitarian_and_human_rights_groups_accusing_Israel_of_genocide_in_Gaza) by international law experts, genocide scholars and human rights organizations [as a genocide](75668427.md).[^cite_note-genocide-60][^cite_note-is_genocide_sources-61]

## History

Main article: [History of the Israeli–Palestinian conflict](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Israeli%E2%80%93Palestinian_conflict)

For a chronological guide, see [Timeline of the Israeli–Palestinian conflict](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_Israeli%E2%80%93Palestinian_conflict).

Further information: [Military operations of the Israeli–Palestinian conflict](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_operations_of_the_Israeli%E2%80%93Palestinian_conflict)





March 1899 correspondence between [Yousef al-Khalidi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yousef_al-Khalidi), [mayor of Jerusalem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mayor_of_Jerusalem) and author of the letter on the left, and [Theodore Herzl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theodore_Herzl), founder of the [Zionist Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zionist_Organization) and author of the letter on the right.



The 1917 [Balfour Declaration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balfour_Declaration) from UK Foreign Secretary [Arthur Balfour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arthur_Balfour) to [Walter Rothschild](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walter_Rothschild,_2nd_Baron_Rothschild) expressing the support of the British government for "the establishment in Palestine of a national home for the Jewish people."

[^cite_note-62]

The Israeli–Palestinian conflict began in the late 19th and early 20th centuries, with the development of political [Zionism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zionism) and the [arrival of Zionist settlers](351513.md) to Palestine.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMorris20081-63][^cite_note-Nur_Masalha-64] During the early 20th century, [Arab nationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_nationalism) and [Turkish nationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_nationalism) also grew within the [Ottoman Empire](22278.md). [World War I](4764461.md) began in 1914, with [Great Britain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Britain) and [France](5843419.md) opposing the Ottoman Empire. To gain support from [Sharif of Mecca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sharif_of_Mecca) [Hussein bin Ali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hussein,_King_of_Hejaz), his Arab [Sharifian Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sharifian_Army), and Arab nationalists in the war against the Ottomans, Great Britain promised in the [McMahon–Hussein correspondence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/McMahon%E2%80%93Hussein_correspondence)[^cite_note-65] that Hussein would have control of an independent state in the Arab areas of the Ottoman Empire.[^cite_note-66] Palestine was "ambiguously omitted" from the Arab state.[^cite_note-\:1-67] The [British Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Empire) supplied a large amount of weapons to the [Arab revolt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_revolt) in 1916–1918.[^cite_note-68] With support from the Arab revolt the British Empire defeated the Ottoman's forces and took control of Palestine, Jordan and Syria. It later transpired that the British and French governments had secretly made the [Sykes-Picot Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sykes-Picot_Agreement)[^cite_note-69] in 1916 that unambiguously excluded Palestine from Arab control and did not allow the formation of an independent Arab state that included it.[^cite_note-\:1-67] [Chaim Weizmann](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chaim_Weizmann)'s efforts to build British support for the Zionist movement would eventually secure the [Balfour Declaration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balfour_Declaration), a public statement issued by the British government in 1917 during the First World War announcing support for the establishment of a "national home for the Jewish people" in Palestine.[^cite_note-70] In July 1920, the short-lived [Arab Kingdom of Syria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Kingdom_of_Syria), with Emir [Faisal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Faisal_I_of_Iraq) (one of the leaders of the Arab revolt) as king and tolerated by Britain, was crushed by French armed forces, equipped with modern artillery.&#x20;

While there was a significant wave of [Jewish immigration in the late 19th century](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Aliyah), it was not until the arrival of more [ideologically Zionist immigrants in the decade preceding the First World War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Aliyah) that the landscape of Ottoman Palestine would start to significantly change.[^cite_note-Ilan_Pappe2-71] [Jewish land purchases](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_land_purchase_in_Palestine) and the eviction of tenant [Arab peasants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fellah) would contribute to the Palestinian population's growing fear of territorial displacement and dispossession in this period, as would armed confrontations in later years.[^cite_note-72] From early on, some in Zionist movement had the idea of "transferring"[^cite_note-73] the Arab Palestinian population out of the land of a future Jewish state.[^cite_note-\:3-74]

According to the political scientist [Norman Finkelstein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Norman_Finkelstein), [population transfer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Population_transfer) was considered as an acceptable solution to the problems of ethnic conflict until around [World War II](32927.md) and even for a time afterward. Transfer was considered a drastic but "often necessary" means to end an ethnic conflict or ethnic [civil war](7085.md).[^cite_note-75] In modern times, transfer is often seen as a euphemism for [ethnic cleansing.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnic_cleansing)

The idea of transfer gained support of the Zionist leadership after it was proposed by the British [Peel Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peel_Commission).[^cite_note-\:3-74] Zionist leaders advocated this for the purpose of establishing a Jewish demographic majority[^cite_note-76] or as a response to violence.[^cite_note-\:4-77][^cite_note-78][^cite_note-79] Israeli historian [Benny Morris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benny_Morris) wrote in 2004 that the idea of transfer was "inevitable and inbuilt into Zionism".[^cite_note-80] He clarified in 2008 that it "had not been part of the original Zionist ideology" but was advocated for starting in the mid-1930s in response to the violence of the Arab Revolt.[^cite_note-\:4-77] There were Arabs that feared displacement as early as the 1880s with one Zionist writing that he had been accused of coming "to drive them out."[^cite_note-81]

### 1920s

With the creation of the [British Mandate in Palestine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Mandate_in_Palestine) after the end of the first world war, large-scale Jewish immigration began accompanied by the development of a separate Jewish-controlled sector of the economy supported by foreign capital.[^cite_note-82] The [British colonial government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Empire) in [Mandatory Palestine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mandatory_Palestine) recognized the [Zionist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zionism) organizations as legitimate and representative self-governing institutions of the Jewish population. These organizations created the structure of a [quasi state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quasi-state).[^cite_note-\:02-83][^cite_note-\:2-84] Chief among them was the Jewish Agency for Palestine (renamed the [Jewish Agency for Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_Agency_for_Israel) after 1948), which would turn into the government of the local Jewish community.[^cite_note-85] It was originally established in 1908 as the [Palestine Office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestine_Office?action=edit\&redlink=1) of the Zionist Organization (now the [World Zionist Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Zionist_Organization)). Britain and the [League of Nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/League_of_Nations) gave it semi-official diplomatic status which increased the [international legitimacy](40229800.md#Background) of it and by extension the Zionist movement.[^cite_note-\:02-83]<sup>: 45 </sup> The [Mandate for Palestine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mandate_for_Palestine) recognized the Jewish Agency "as a public body for the purpose of advising and cooperating with the Administration of Palestine." It was also entitled to diplomatic representation in the League of Nations [Permanent Mandates Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Permanent_Mandates_Commission) in Geneva, London and elsewhere.[^cite_note-\:02-83]

There is agreement between Israeli historian [Benny Morris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benny_Morris) and Palestinian historian [Rashid Khalidi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rashid_Khalidi) that the Arab community didn't have same level of governance and recognition, but there is disagreement as to why. Khalid believes the British colonial government did not similarly recognize the Arab majority in Palestine, which it referred to at times as "non-Jewish communities," and which did not have their own international recognition[^cite_note-\:02-83][^cite_note-\:2-84] and that they referred to the [Palestine Arab Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestine_Arab_Congress) as unrepresentative.[^cite_note-86] Morris believes Palestine's Arabs in this period were characterized by a "vying coalition of clans" that setup "political parties" for "form's sake."[^cite_note-87]

[Amin al-Husseini](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amin_al-Husseini), was appointed as [Grand Mufti of Jerusalem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_Mufti_of_Jerusalem) by British [High Commissioner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High_Commissioner) [Herbert Samuel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herbert_Samuel,_1st_Viscount_Samuel). Husseini immediately marked the [Jewish national movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zionism) and [Jewish immigration to Palestine](351513.md) as the enemies of his cause.[^cite_note-al-Husseini-88] He initiated large-scale riots against the Jews as early as 1920 [in Jerusalem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1920_Nebi_Musa_riots), and in 1921 [in Jaffa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jaffa_riots_\(May_1921\)). Among the results of this violence was the establishment of the [Haganah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haganah) Jewish paramilitary force. In 1929 a further series of violent [riots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1929_Palestine_riots) resulted in the deaths of 133 Jews and 116 Arabs, with significant Jewish casualties in [Hebron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1929_Hebron_massacre) and [Safed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1929_Palestine_riots#Safed_massacre,_29_August), and the evacuation of Jews from Hebron and Gaza.[^cite_note-SelaConflict-89] Alongside political unrest, rural Palestine in the Mandate era experienced demographic and agricultural expansion. Large villages such as [Lajjun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lajjun) and [Hamama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hamama) grew in size, [reclaimed marginal lands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_dune_agriculture), and integrated into regional markets, reflecting the resilience and transformation of the Palestinian countryside during this period.[^cite_note-90][^cite_note-91][^cite_note-92]

### 1936–1939 Arab revolt

Main article: [1936–1939 Arab revolt in Palestine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1936%E2%80%931939_Arab_revolt_in_Palestine)



British colonial [Palestine Police Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestine_Police_Force) dispersing a crowd in the [Jaffa riots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jaffa_riots_\(April_1936\)), seen as the start of the [Arab revolt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1936%E2%80%931939_Arab_revolt_in_Palestine) (1936–1939) in Palestine against British rule and the mass [Jewish immigration](351513.md) and rapid expansion of Jewish settlements it allowed.

[^cite_note-\:0-93]



Palestinian [Arab-Christian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Christian)-owned newspaper [*Falastin*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falastin_\(newspaper\)), 18 June 1936, caricatured [Zionism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zionism) as a crocodile, protected by a British officer, telling Palestinian Arabs: "Don't be afraid! I will swallow you peacefully..."

[^cite_note-94]

The peasant-led [popular uprising](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Popular_uprising) was a [national](47246228.md) struggle of Palestinian Arabs against British [^cite_note-\:0-93]rule and against the rapid expansion of Jewish settlements that it allowed.[^cite_note-\:0-93] It occurred during a [peak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fifth_Aliyah) in the influx of European Jewish immigrants,[^cite_note-95] as rural *[fellahin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fellah)* faced growing plight and land dispossession, contributing to [rural flight](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rural_flight) and [urbanization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urbanization), which eroded traditional social bonds and failed to alleviate the peasants' abject poverty.[^cite_note-\:0-93][^cite_note-96][^cite_note-98]

In the early 1930s, the Arab national struggle in Palestine had drawn many Arab nationalist militants from across the Middle East, such as [Sheikh Izz ad-Din al-Qassam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Izz_ad-Din_al-Qassam) from Syria, who established the [Black Hand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Hand_\(Mandatory_Palestine\)) militant group and had prepared the grounds for the [1936–1939 Arab revolt in Palestine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1936%E2%80%931939_Arab_revolt_in_Palestine). Following the death of al-Qassam at the hands of the British in late 1935, tensions erupted in 1936 into the [Arab general strike](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_general_strike) and general boycott. The strike soon deteriorated into violence, and the Arab revolt was repressed by the British armed forces with assistance from auxiliary units of the [Palestine Police Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestine_Police_Force) (the [Jewish Settlement Police](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_Settlement_Police), the [Jewish Supernumerary Police](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_Supernumerary_Police), and the [Special Night Squads](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special_Night_Squads)). Palestinian historian [Rashid Khalidi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rashid_Khalidi) estimates suppression of the revolt left at least 10% of the adult male population killed, wounded, imprisoned or exiled.[^cite_note-99] Other estimates of deaths caused by suppression of the revolt are lower, with official British figures and several Israeli historians viewing many of the Arab deaths in this period as attributable to other causes.[^cite_note-100] Between the expulsion of much of the Arab leadership and the weakening of the economy, the Palestinians would struggle to confront the growing Zionist movement.[^cite_note-101]

The cost and risks associated with the revolt and the ongoing [inter-communal conflict](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intercommunal_conflict_in_Mandatory_Palestine) led to a shift in British policies in the region and the appointment of the [Peel Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peel_Commission) which recommended creation of a small Jewish country, which the two main Zionist leaders, [Chaim Weizmann](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chaim_Weizmann) and [David Ben-Gurion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Ben-Gurion), accepted. Both of their writings indicated they thought that if they accepted a small state it would expand later.[^cite_note-102][^cite_note-103] The subsequent [White Paper of 1939](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_Paper_of_1939), which rejected a Jewish state and sought to limit Jewish immigration to the region, was the breaking point in relations between British authorities and the Zionist movement.[^cite_note-104][^cite_note-105][^cite_note-106]

### 1940–1947

Further information: [Intercommunal conflict in Mandatory Palestine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intercommunal_conflict_in_Mandatory_Palestine) and [Jewish insurgency in Mandatory Palestine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_insurgency_in_Mandatory_Palestine)



[Haganah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haganah) ship *Jewish State* carrying illegal Jewish immigrants from Europe at the [Haifa Port](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_Haifa), [Mandatory Palestine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mandatory_Palestine), 1947

Renewed violence, which continued sporadically until the beginning of World War II, ended with around 5,000 casualties on the Arab side and 700 casualties combined on the British and Jewish sides in total.[^cite_note-107][^cite_note-108] With the eruption of [World War II](32927.md), the situation in Mandatory Palestine calmed down. In this period, there was a shift towards a more moderate stance among Palestinian Arabs under the leadership of the [Nashashibi clan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nashashibi_family). Even a joint Jewish–Arab [Palestine Regiment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestine_Regiment) was established, under British command, that fought against Nazi German troops in North Africa. The more radical exiled al-Husseini faction, however, tended to cooperate with Nazi Germany. It participated in the establishment of pro-Nazi propaganda machinery throughout the Arab world. The [defeat of Arab nationalists in Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglo-Iraqi_War) and subsequent relocation of al-Husseini to Nazi-occupied Europe tied his hands regarding field operations in Palestine, though he regularly demanded that the Italians and the Germans [bomb Tel Aviv](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italian_bombing_of_Mandatory_Palestine_in_World_War_II).[^cite_note-SelaConflict-89] The [Jewish Agency for Palestine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_Agency_for_Israel) and [Palestinian National Defense Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Defense_Party_\(Mandatory_Palestine\)) called on Palestine's Jewish and Arab youths to volunteer for the British Army. 30,000 Palestinian Jews and 12,000 Palestinian Arabs enlisted in the British armed forces during the war, while a [Jewish Brigade](887206.md) was created in 1944.[^cite_note-109]

By the end of World War II, a crisis over the fate of [Holocaust survivors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holocaust_survivors) from Europe led to renewed tensions between the [Yishuv](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yishuv) and Mandate authorities. By 1944, Jewish groups began to conduct military-style operations against the British, with the aim of persuading Great Britain to accept the formation of a Jewish state. This culminated in the [Jewish insurgency in Mandatory Palestine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_insurgency_in_Mandatory_Palestine). The sustained Zionist paramilitary campaign against the British authorities, along with diplomatic damage and worldwide negative publicity from treating Jewish refugees as illegal immigrants, were major factors in the British decision to leave.[^cite_note-110] To decide what to do next, the [United Nations Special Committee on Palestine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Special_Committee_on_Palestine) was established which recommended partition and the [End of the British Mandate for Palestine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/End_of_the_British_Mandate_for_Palestine).[^cite_note-111]

&#x20;\
&#x20;The 1948 Palestine war refers to the first stage [1947–1948 civil war in Mandatory Palestine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1947%E2%80%931948_civil_war_in_Mandatory_Palestine) and the second stage [1948 Arab–Israeli War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1948_Arab%E2%80%93Israeli_War). The preceding civil war is also sometimes included by those referring to the 1948 Arab–Israeli War.

#### 1947 United Nations partition plan and civil war

Main articles: [United Nations Partition Plan for Palestine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Partition_Plan_for_Palestine) and [1947–1948 civil war in Mandatory Palestine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1947%E2%80%931948_civil_war_in_Mandatory_Palestine)



Distribution of Palestine land ownership in 1945



Population distribution in Palestine in 1947

Two maps reviewed by the United Nations in considering partition, including estimates of the [region demographics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demographic_history_of_Palestine_\(region\)).

[^cite_note-112]

[^cite_note-113]

On 29 November 1947, the [General Assembly of the United Nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Assembly_of_the_United_Nations) adopted [Resolution 181(II)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Partition_Plan_for_Palestine)[^cite_note-114] that recommending the adoption and implementation of a plan to partition Palestine into an Arab state, a Jewish state and an international City of Jerusalem.[^cite_note-Baum-115] Palestinian Arabs were opposed to the partition.[^cite_note-116] [The Yishuv](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yishuv) accepted the partition but began to think in terms of expanding Israel's borders beyond what was allocated to it by the UN. One leader said they would capture more territory if a war was launched against them.[^cite_note-117] On the next day, Palestine was swept by violence, igniting [1947–1948 civil war in Mandatory Palestine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1947%E2%80%931948_civil_war_in_Mandatory_Palestine), the first phase of the [1948 Palestine war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1948_Palestine_war). For four months, under continuous Arab provocation and attack, the Yishuv was usually on the defensive while occasionally retaliating.[^cite_note-118] The [Arab League](52625.md) helped the Palestinian Arabs' struggle by forming the volunteer-based [Arab Liberation Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Liberation_Army) and supporting the Palestinian Arab [Holy War Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Army_of_the_Holy_War), under the leadership of [Abd al-Qadir al-Husayni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abd_al-Qadir_al-Husayni) and [Hasan Salama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hasan_Salama). On the Jewish side, the civil war was managed by the major underground militias – the [Haganah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haganah), [Irgun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irgun) and [Lehi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lehi_\(group\)) – strengthened by numerous Jewish veterans of World War II and foreign volunteers. By spring 1948, it was already clear that the Arab forces were nearing a total collapse, while Yishuv forces gained more and more territory, creating a large scale [refugee problem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1948_Palestinian_exodus) of Palestinian Arabs.[^cite_note-SelaConflict-89]

#### 1948 Arab–Israeli War

Main article: [1948 Arab–Israeli War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1948_Arab%E2%80%93Israeli_War)

Following the end of the British Mandate and the [Declaration of the Establishment of the State of Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Declaration_of_the_Establishment_of_the_State_of_Israel) on 14 May 1948, the Arab League decided to intervene on behalf of the Palestinian Arabs. They marched their forces into the former British Palestine, beginning the 1948 Arab–Israeli War.[^cite_note-Baum-115] The overall fighting, leading to around 15,000 casualties, resulted in cease-fire and armistice agreements of 1949, with Israel holding much of the former Mandate territory, Jordan occupying and later annexing the [West Bank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jordanian_annexation_of_the_West_Bank) and Egypt taking over the Gaza Strip, where the [All-Palestine Government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/All-Palestine_Government) was declared by the Arab League on 23 September 1948.[^cite_note-119] The [1947–1948 civil war in Mandatory Palestine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1947%E2%80%931948_civil_war_in_Mandatory_Palestine) and 1948 Arab-Israeli War resulted in the [ethnic cleansing of 750,000 Palestinian Arabs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nakba).[^cite_note-Ilan_Pappe3-52] In 1950, Israel passed the [Law of Return](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Law_of_Return), which allowed Jews and their spouses to become citizens with voting rights, even if they had never lived there before.&#x20;

During the period between 1948 and 1967, no independent Palestinian Arab state was established in the West Bank and in the Gaza Strip territories, then controlled by Jordan and Egypt. [Rashid Khalidi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rashid_Khalidi) describes the post-1948 period as one in which Palestinians were dispersed, divided, and largely deprived of independent political agency, while Israel and the Arab states controlled the territory of former Mandatory Palestine.[^cite_note-120] Jordan annexed the West Bank in 1950 and treated independent Palestinian organization as a threat, while Egyptian authorities in Gaza allowed only limited Palestinian political activity.[^cite_note-\:5-121] During this period, Palestinian activism often developed within broader Arab and transnational political movements, including Pan-Arab currents, before the emergence of a more autonomous Palestinian national movement in the 1960s.[^cite_note-122][^cite_note-123] Khalidi writes that the Arab League founded the [Palestine Liberation Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestine_Liberation_Organization) (PLO) in 1964 partly "to keep control of the Palestinian arena and head off burgeoning Palestinian activism."[^cite_note-\:5-121] Article 24 of the original 1964 Palestinian National Charter stated that the PLO "does not exercise any territorial sovereignty over the West Bank in the [Hashemite Kingdom of Jordan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hashemite_Kingdom_of_Jordan), on the Gaza Strip or in the Himmah Area";<https://www.files.ethz.ch/isn/125413/2123_Palestinian_National_Charter.pdf> this provision was removed in the revised 1968 charter.[^cite_note-124]

### 1956 Suez Crisis

Main article: [Suez Crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suez_Crisis)

Through the 1950s, Jordan and Egypt supported the [Palestinian Fedayeen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_Fedayeen) militants' cross-border attacks into Israel, while Israel carried out [its own reprisal operations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reprisal_operations) in the host countries. The 1956 [Suez Crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suez_Crisis) resulted in a short-term Israeli occupation of the Gaza Strip and exile of the [All-Palestine Government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/All-Palestine_Government), which was later restored with Israeli withdrawal. The All-Palestine Government was completely abandoned by Egypt in 1959 and was officially merged into the [United Arab Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Arab_Republic), to the detriment of the Palestinian national movement. [Gaza Strip](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egyptian_occupation_of_the_Gaza_Strip) then was put under the authority of the Egyptian military administrator, making it a de facto military occupation. In 1964, however, a new organization, the Palestine Liberation Organization (PLO), was established by Yasser Arafat.[^cite_note-Baum-115] It immediately won the support of most Arab League governments and was granted a seat in the [Arab League](52625.md).

### 1967 Six-Day War

Main article: [Six-Day War](29328.md)



During the Six-Day War in 1967, Israel captured the [West Bank](33209.md), the [Gaza Strip](12047.md), the [Golan Heights](7515865.md) and the [Sinai Peninsula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sinai_Peninsula). Each of these territories except the Sinai remain under [Israeli occupation](2781576.md).

In the [1967 Arab-Israel War](29328.md), Israel occupied the Palestinian West Bank, East Jerusalem, Gaza Strip, Egyptian Sinai, Syrian Golan Heights, and two islands in the [Gulf of Aqaba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulf_of_Aqaba).

The war exerted a significant effect upon Palestinian nationalism, as the PLO was unable to establish any control on the ground and ultimately established its headquarters in Jordan, from where it supported the Jordanian army during the [War of Attrition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_of_Attrition). However, the Palestinian base in Jordan collapsed with the [Jordanian–Palestinian civil war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_September_in_Jordan) in 1970, after which the PLO was forced to relocate to South Lebanon.

In the mid-1970s, the international community converged on a framework to resolve the conflict through the establishment of an independent Palestinian state in the West Bank and Gaza. This "[land for peace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Land_for_peace)" proposal was endorsed by the [ICJ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ICJ) and [UN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UN).[^cite_note-125]

### 1973 Yom Kippur War

Main article: [Yom Kippur War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yom_Kippur_War)

On 6 October 1973, a coalition of Arab forces consisting of mainly Egypt and Syria launched a surprise attack against Israel on the Jewish holy day of Yom Kippur. Despite this, the war concluded with an Israeli victory, with both sides suffering tremendous casualties.

Following the end of the war, the UN Security Council passed Resolution 338 confirming the land-for-peace principle established in Resolution 242, initiating the Middle East peace process. The Arab defeat would play an important role in the PLO's willingness to pursue a negotiated settlement to the conflict,[^cite_note-126][^cite_note-127] while many Israelis began to believe that the area under Israeli occupation could not be held indefinitely by force.[^cite_note-128][^cite_note-129]

The Camp David Accords, agreed upon by Israel and Egypt in 1978, primarily aimed to establish a peace treaty between the two countries. The accords also proposed the creation of a "Self-Governing Authority" for the Arab population in the West Bank and Gaza Strip, excluding Jerusalem, under Israeli control. A peace treaty based on these accords was signed in 1979, leading to Israel's withdrawal from the occupied Egyptian Sinai Peninsula by 1982.[^cite_note-130][^cite_note-131]

### 1982 Lebanon War

Main article: [1982 Lebanon War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1982_Lebanon_War)

During the [Lebanese Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lebanese_Civil_War), Palestinian militants continued to launch attacks against Israel while also battling opponents within Lebanon. In 1978, the [Coastal Road massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coastal_Road_massacre) led to the Israeli full-scale invasion known as [Operation Litani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Litani). This operation sought to dislodge the PLO from Lebanon while expanding the area under the control of the Israeli allied Christian militias in southern Lebanon. The operation succeeded in leaving a large portion of the south in control of the Israeli proxy which would eventually form the [South Lebanon Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Lebanon_Army). Under United States pressure, Israeli forces would eventually withdraw from Lebanon.[^cite_note-132][^cite_note-133][^cite_note-134][^cite_note-135]

In 1982, Israel, having secured its southern border with Egypt, sought to resolve the Palestinian issue by attempting to dismantle the military and political power of the PLO in Lebanon.[^cite_note-136] The goal was to establish a friendly regime in Lebanon and continue its policy of settlement and annexation in occupied Palestine.[^cite_note-137][^cite_note-Shalim_2015_Lebanese_Quagmire-138][^cite_note-139] The PLO had observed the latest ceasefire with Israel and shown a preference for negotiations over military operations. As a result, Israel sought to remove the PLO as a potential negotiating partner.[^cite_note-140][^cite_note-141][^cite_note-142] Most Palestinian militants were defeated within several weeks, Beirut was captured, and the PLO headquarters were evacuated to Tunisia in June by Yasser Arafat's decision.

### First Intifada (1987–1993)

Main article: [First Intifada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Intifada)



Protests in the Gaza Strip in the early days of the First Intifada in 1987.

For causes that are not agreed on beyond political frustration, the first Palestinian uprising began in 1987. Palestinian historian Rashid Khalidi compares it to the 1936-1939 Arab Revolt. He views it as a grassroots popular protest that started in response to a collision involving an Israeli army vehicle and also as a result of the suppression of the Palestinian national movement. He points to the large involvement of youth and women taking leadership roles in normally male dominated politics, to illustrate its popularity.[^cite_note-143] American political scientist Mary King believes it was a response to Israel's military occupation, escalating attacks on Palestinians, and policies of settlement building and collective punishment and that uprising largely consisted of nonviolent acts of civil disobedience and protest.[^cite_note-King-144] Israeli historian Benny Morris points to the death of an Arab schoolgirl and the wounding of two others by stay bullets in the preceding weeks as well as a rising numbers of clashes in the preceding years. He views the Intifada as largely unarmed rather than nonviolent, noting the use of knives and other improvised weapons, as well as the hundreds of casualties in the first month.[^cite_note-145]

By the early 1990s, the conflict, termed the [First Intifada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Intifada), was the focus of international settlement efforts, in part motivated by the success of the Egyptian–Israeli peace treaty of 1982. Eventually the [Israeli–Palestinian peace process](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israeli%E2%80%93Palestinian_peace_process) led to the [Oslo Accords](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oslo_Accords) of 1993, allowing the PLO to relocate from Tunisia and take ground in the [West Bank](33209.md) and [Gaza Strip](12047.md), establishing the [Palestinian National Authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_National_Authority). The peace process also had significant opposition among elements of Palestinian society, such as Hamas and Palestinian Islamic Jihad, who immediately initiated a campaign of attacks targeting Israelis. Following hundreds of casualties and a wave of anti-government propaganda, Israeli Prime Minister [Rabin was assassinated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assassination_of_Yitzhak_Rabin) by an [Israeli far-right extremist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Far-right_politics_in_Israel) who objected to the peace initiative. This struck a serious blow to the peace process, which in 1996 led to the newly elected government of Israel backing off from the process, to some degree.[^cite_note-SelaConflict-89]

### Second Intifada (2000–2005)

Main article: [Second Intifada](71717.md)



Demonstrations against the construction of the [West Bank separation barrier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Bank_barrier) in the city of [Bil'in](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bil'in), occupied West Bank, in 2005 during the Second Intifada.

Following several years of unsuccessful negotiations, the conflict re-erupted as the [Second Intifada](71717.md) in September 2000. The violence, escalating into an open conflict between the [Palestinian National Security Forces](5844475.md) and the [Israel Defense Forces](39237.md), lasted until 2004/2005 and led to thousands of fatalities. In 2005, Israeli Prime Minister Sharon ordered the [removal of Israeli settlers and soldiers from Gaza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israeli_disengagement_from_Gaza). Israel and its Supreme Court formally declared an end to occupation, saying it "had no effective control over what occurred" in Gaza.[^cite_note-cnn2009-01-06-146] However, the [United Nations](31769.md), [Human Rights Watch](46530.md) and many other international bodies and [NGOs](46539.md) continue to consider Israel to be the occupying power of the Gaza Strip as Israel controls Gaza Strip's airspace, territorial waters and controls the movement of people or goods in or out of Gaza by air or sea.[^cite_note-cnn2009-01-06-146][^cite_note-AI_briefing-147][^cite_note-isrlpa13698-148]

### Fatah–Hamas split (2006–2007)

Main article: [Fatah–Hamas conflict](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fatah%E2%80%93Hamas_conflict)

In 2006, Hamas won a plurality of 44% in the [Palestinian parliamentary election](38945340.md). Israel responded it would begin [economic sanctions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2006%E2%80%9307_economic_sanctions_against_the_Palestinian_National_Authority) unless Hamas agreed to accept prior Israeli–Palestinian agreements, forswear violence, and recognize Israel's right to exist, all of which Hamas rejected.[^cite_note-149] After internal Palestinian political struggle between Fatah and Hamas erupted into the [2007 Battle of Gaza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Gaza_\(2007\)), Hamas took full control of the area.[^cite_note-150] In 2007, Israel imposed a naval [blockade on the Gaza Strip](19224310.md), and cooperation with Egypt allowed a ground blockade of the Egyptian border.



Aftermath of a Hamas suicide bombing on a bus in Tel Aviv.

The tensions between Israel and Hamas escalated until late 2008, when Israel launched operation [Cast Lead](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cast_Lead) upon Gaza, resulting in thousands of civilian casualties and billions of dollars in damage. By February 2009, a ceasefire was signed with international mediation between the parties, though the occupation and small and sporadic eruptions of violence continued.

In 2011, a Palestinian Authority attempt to gain UN membership as a fully sovereign state failed. In Hamas-controlled Gaza, sporadic rocket attacks on Israel and Israeli air raids continued to occur.[^cite_note-151][^cite_note-152][^cite_note-153][^cite_note-154] In November 2012, Palestinian representation in the UN was upgraded to a non-member observer state, and its mission title was changed from "Palestine (represented by PLO)" to "[State of Palestine](241405.md)". In 2014, [another war](43243199.md) broke out between Israel and Gaza, resulting in over 70 Israeli and over 2,000 Palestinian casualties.[^cite_note-155]

### Gaza war (2023–present)

Main article: [Gaza war](74998519.md)



Map of the [Gaza war](74998519.md) in Gaza and southern Israel

After the 2014 war and [2021 crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_Israel%E2%80%93Palestine_crisis), Hamas began planning an attack on Israel.[^cite_note-156] In 2022, Netanyahu returned to power while headlining a hardline [far-right](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Far-right_politics_in_Israel) [government](72545811.md),[^cite_note-157] which led to greater political strife in Israel[^cite_note-158] and clashes in the Palestinian territories.[^cite_note-159] This culminated in [a surprise attack](74998317.md) launched by Hamas-led militant groups on southern Israel from the Gaza Strip on 7 October 2023, in which more than 1,195 Israeli civilians, military personnel, and other foreign nationals were killed, and [251 were taken hostage into Gaza](75025451.md).[^cite_note-160][^cite_note-161] The Israeli military responded by [declaring war](74998519.md) on Hamas and conducting an extensive [aerial bombardment](323167.md) campaign on Gaza,[^cite_note-162] followed by a [large-scale ground invasion](74998519.md) with the stated goal of destroying Hamas, freeing hostages, and controlling security in Gaza afterwards.[^cite_note-163] South Africa [accused](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa_v._Israel_\(Genocide_Convention\)) Israel of [genocide](75668427.md) at the [International Court of Justice](14918.md) and called for an immediate ceasefire.[^cite_note-ICJ_SA_proceedings_vs_IL_29Dec2023-164] The court issued an order requiring Israel to take all measures to prevent any acts contrary to the [1948 Genocide Convention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1948_Genocide_Convention),[^cite_note-Patrick_Kingsley-165][^cite_note-166][^cite_note-Rajvanshi26Jan2024-167] but did not order Israel to suspend its military campaign.[^cite_note-168]

The war [spilled over the Middle East](75895273.md), with Israel engaging in clashes with local militias [in the West Bank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israeli_incursions_in_the_West_Bank_\(2023%E2%80%93present\)), [Hezbollah](13919.md) [in Lebanon and northern Israel](75005425.md), and other Iranian-backed militias in Syria. Israel and the United States have also engaged with Iran itself, first in the [Twelve-Day War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twelve-Day_War) in 2025 and again in the [2026 Iran war](82533582.md).[^cite_note-169][^cite_note-170][^cite_note-171][^cite_note-172] Iranian-backed militias engaged in [clashes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Attacks_on_US_bases_during_the_Gaza_war) with the United States,[^cite_note-173] while the [Houthis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Houthi_movement) [blockaded](75207766.md) the Red Sea in protest,[^cite_note-174] to which the United States responded with airstrikes in [Yemen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/US%E2%80%93UK_airstrikes_on_Yemen),[^cite_note-175] [Iraq, and Syria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/February_2024_United_States_airstrikes_in_Iraq_and_Syria).[^cite_note-176] Taking advantage of the weakening position of Iranian-backed militias, [Syrian opposition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian_opposition_\(2011%E2%80%932024\)) groups initiated [an offensive](78460726.md) in November 2024 that reignited the [Syrian civil war](30741795.md), culminating in the [fall of the Assad regime](78548066.md) and the establishment of [a transitional government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian_caretaker_government) in the place of [the former Ba'athist government](57134800.md); Israel [invaded](78549398.md) the area around [its demarcated boundaries with Syria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israel%E2%80%93Syria_border) shortly afterwards.[^cite_note-cnn20241208a-177]



Aerial view showing the destruction of [Rafah](584373.md) in January 2025

The war has caused widespread destruction, a [humanitarian crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gaza_humanitarian_crisis_\(2023%E2%80%93present\)), and an [ongoing famine](75828824.md) in the Gaza Strip,[^cite_note-Dannenbaum_Dill_2024-58][^cite_note-IPC_22_August_2025-59] while most of the population was [forcibly displaced](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gaza_Strip_evacuations).[^cite_note-Dannenbaum_Dill_2024-58] Various human rights organizations and scholars of [genocide studies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genocide_studies) and [international law](8195726.md) have concluded that Israel's actions in Gaza constitute genocide,[^cite_note-genocide-60][^cite_note-is_genocide_sources-61][^cite_note-178] though others dispute this.[^cite_note-not_genocide_sources-179] Over 70,000 Palestinians in Gaza [have been killed](75130785.md), almost half of them [women](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Women_in_the_Gaza_war#Gaza_Strip) and [children](75987631.md), and more than 148,000 have been injured.[^cite_note-180][^cite_note-181][^cite_note-182] A study in *[The Lancet](463899.md)* estimated 64,260 deaths in Gaza from [traumatic injuries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Traumatic_injuries) by June 2024, while noting a potentially larger death toll when "indirect" deaths are included.[^cite_note-Lancet2025-183][^cite_note-184][^cite_note-185][^cite_note-186] As of May 2025, a comparable figure for traumatic injury deaths would be 93,000.[^cite_note-187]

A ceasefire has *de jure* been in effect since 10 October 2025 as part of a [peace plan](81215079.md). The last remaining living Israeli hostages were returned that month, and the last body was recovered in January 2026. However, limited warfare in Gaza has continued as of September 2026.[^cite_note-188]

## Attempts to reach a peaceful settlement

The PLO's participation in diplomatic negotiations was dependent on its complete disavowal of terrorism and recognition of Israel's "right to exist." This stipulation required the PLO to abandon its objective of reclaiming all of historic Palestine and instead focus on the 22 percent which came under Israeli military control in 1967.[^cite_note-hc_baconi-189] By the late 1970s, Palestinian leadership in the occupied territories and most Arab states supported a two-state settlement.[^cite_note-mishal_sela-190] In 1981, Saudi Arabia put forward a plan based on a two-state settlement to the conflict with support from the Arab League.[^cite_note-191] Israeli analyst Avner Yaniv describes Arafat as ready to make a historic compromise at this time, while the Israeli cabinet continued to oppose the existence of a Palestinian state. Yaniv described Arafat's willingness to compromise as a "peace offensive" which Israel responded to by planning to remove the PLO as a potential negotiating partner in order to evade international diplomatic pressure.[^cite_note-Avner_Yaniv-192] Israel would invade Lebanon the following year in an attempt to undermine the PLO as a political organization, weakening Palestinian nationalism and facilitating the annexation of the West Bank into Greater Israel.[^cite_note-Shalim_2015_Lebanese_Quagmire-138]

While the PLO had adopted a program of pursuing a Palestinian state alongside Israel since the mid-1970s, the 1988 [Palestinian Declaration of Independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_Declaration_of_Independence) formally consecrated this objective. This declaration, which was based on resolutions from the Palestine National Council sessions in the late 1970s and 1980s, advocated for the creation of a Palestinian state comprising the West Bank, Gaza Strip, and East Jerusalem, within the borders set by the 1949 armistice lines prior to 5 June 1967. Following the declaration, Arafat explicitly denounced all forms of terrorism and affirmed the PLO's acceptance of UN Resolutions 242 and 338, as well as the recognition of Israel's right to exist. All the conditions defined by Henry Kissinger for US negotiations with the PLO had now been met.[^cite_note-193]

Then-Israeli prime minister [Yitzhak Shamir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yitzhak_Shamir) stood behind the stance that the PLO was a terrorist organization. He maintained a strict stance against any concessions, including withdrawal from occupied Palestinian territories, recognition of or negotiations with the PLO, and especially the establishment of a Palestinian state. Shamir viewed the U.S. decision to engage in dialogue with the PLO as a mistake that threatened the existing territorial status quo. He argued that negotiating with the PLO meant accepting the existence of a Palestinian state and hence was unacceptable.[^cite_note-194]

The term "peace process" refers to the step-by-step approach to resolving the conflict. Having originally entered into usage to describe the US mediated negotiations between Israel and surrounding Arab countries, notably Egypt, the term "peace-process" has grown to be associated with an emphasis on the negotiation process rather than on presenting a comprehensive solution to the conflict.[^cite_note-Pappé_2022-195][^cite_note-William_B._Quandt-196][^cite_note-Khalidi_2020-197] As part of this process, fundamental issues of the Israeli–Palestinian conflict such as borders, access to resources, and the Palestinian right of return, have been left to "final status" talks. Such "final status" negotiations along the lines discussed in Madrid in 1991 have never taken place.[^cite_note-Khalidi_2020-197]

The Oslo Accords of 1993 and 1995 built on the incremental framework put in place by the 1978 Camp David negotiations and the 1991 Madrid and Washington talks. The motivation behind the incremental approach towards a settlement was that it would "build confidence", but the eventual outcome was instead a dramatic decline in mutual confidence. At each incremental stage, Israel further entrenched its occupation of the Palestinian territories, despite the PA upholding its obligation to curbing violent attacks from extremist groups, in part by cooperating with Israeli forces.[^cite_note-mwe_slater-198]

[Meron Benvenisti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meron_Benvenisti), former deputy mayor of Jerusalem, observed that life became harsher for Palestinians during this period as state violence increased and Palestinian land continued to be expropriated as settlements expanded.[^cite_note-199][^cite_note-pop_christison-200][^cite_note-homme-201][^cite_note-mwoe-202] Israeli foreign minister Shlomo Ben-Ami described the Oslo Accords as legitimizing "the transformation of the West Bank into what has been called a 'cartographic cheeseboard'."[^cite_note-sowwop-203]

#### Creation of the Palestinian Authority and security cooperation

Core to the Oslo Accords was the creation of the Palestinian Authority and the security cooperation it would enter into with the Israeli military authorities in what has been described as the "outsourcing" of the occupation to the PA.[^cite_note-hc_baconi-189] Just before signing the Oslo accord, [Rabin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yitzhak_Rabin) described the expectation that the "Palestinians will be better at establishing internal security than we were, because they will allow no appeals to the Supreme Court and will prevent \[human rights groups] from criticizing the conditions there."[^cite_note-204] Along these lines, Ben-Ami, who participated in the Camp David 2000 talks, described this process: "One of the meanings of Oslo was that the PLO was eventually Israel's collaborator in the task of stifling the Intifada and cutting short what was clearly an authentically democratic struggle for Palestinian independence."[^cite_note-sowwop-203]

The [Wye River Memorandum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wye_River_Memorandum) agreed on by the PA and Israel introduced a "zero tolerance" policy for "terror and violence." This policy was uniformly criticized by human rights organizations for its "encouragement" of human rights abuses.[^cite_note-205][^cite_note-206] [Dennis Ross](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dennis_Ross) describes the Wye as having successfully reduced both violent and non-violent protests, both of which he considers to be "inconsistent with the spirit of Wye."[^cite_note-207] Watson claims that the PA frequently violated its obligations to curb incitement[^cite_note-208] and its record on curbing terrorism and other security obligations under the [Wye River Memorandum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wye_River_Memorandum) was, at best, mixed.[^cite_note-209]

### Oslo Accords (1993, 1995)

Main article: [Oslo Accords](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oslo_Accords)



A [peace movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peace_movement) poster: [Israeli](459004.md) and [Palestinian flags](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_flag) and the word *peace* in [Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_language) and [Hebrew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hebrew_language)



[Yitzhak Rabin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yitzhak_Rabin), [Bill Clinton](3356.md), and [Yasser Arafat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yasser_Arafat) during the Oslo Accords on 13 September 1993

In 1993, Israeli officials led by [Yitzhak Rabin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yitzhak_Rabin) and Palestinian leaders from the [Palestine Liberation Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestine_Liberation_Organization) led by [Yasser Arafat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yasser_Arafat) strove to find a peaceful solution through what became known as the Oslo peace process. A crucial milestone in this process was Arafat's letter of recognition of Israel's right to exist. Emblematic of the asymmetry in the Oslo process, Israel was not required to, and did not, recognize the right of a Palestinian state to exist. In 1993, the [Declaration of Principles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Declaration_of_Principles) (or Oslo I) was signed and set forward a framework for future Israeli–Palestinian negotiations, in which key issues would be left to "final status" talks. The stipulations of the Oslo agreements ran contrary to the international consensus for resolving the conflict; the agreements did not uphold Palestinian self-determination or statehood and repealed the internationally accepted interpretation of [UN Resolution 242](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UN_Resolution_242) that land cannot be acquired by war.[^cite_note-pop_christison-200] With respect to access to land and resources, Noam Chomsky described the Oslo agreements as allowing "Israel to do virtually what it likes."[^cite_note-ft_Chomsky-210] The Oslo process was delicate and progressed in fits and starts.

The process took a turning point at the [assassination of Yitzhak Rabin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assassination_of_Yitzhak_Rabin) in November 1995 and the election of Netanyahu in 1996, finally unraveling when Arafat and [Ehud Barak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ehud_Barak) failed to reach an agreement at Camp David in July 2000 and later at Taba in 2001.[^cite_note-Pappé_2022-195][^cite_note-giim_norman-211] The interim period specified by Oslo had not built confidence between the two parties; Barak had failed to implement additional stages of the interim agreements and settlements expanded by 10% during his short term.[^cite_note-p_kimmerling-212] The disagreement between the two parties at Camp David was primarily on the acceptance (or rejection) of international consensus.[^cite_note-pwh_ben-ami-213][^cite_note-214] For Palestinian negotiators, the international consensus, as represented by the yearly vote in the UN General Assembly which passes almost unanimously, was the starting point for negotiations. The Israeli negotiators, supported by the American participants, did not accept the international consensus as the basis for a settlement.[^cite_note-215] Both sides eventually accepted the Clinton parameters "with reservations" but the talks at Taba were "called to a halt" by Barak, and the peace process itself came to a stand-still.[^cite_note-giim_norman-211] Ben-Ami, who participated in the talks at Camp David as Israel's foreign minister, would later describe the proposal on the table: "The Clinton parameters... are the best proof that Arafat was right to turn down the summit's offers".[^cite_note-pwh_ben-ami-213]

### Camp David Summit (2000)

Main article: [2000 Camp David Summit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2000_Camp_David_Summit)

In July 2000, US President Bill Clinton convened a peace summit between Palestinian President Yasser Arafat and Israeli Prime Minister Ehud Barak. Barak reportedly put forward the following as "bases for negotiation", via the US to the Palestinian President: a non-militarized Palestinian state split into 3–4 parts containing 87–92% of the West Bank after having already given up 78% of historic Palestine.[^cite_note-216] Thus, an Israeli offer of 91 percent of the West Bank (5,538 km<sup>2</sup> of the West Bank translates into only 86 percent from the Palestinian perspective),[^cite_note-217] including Arab parts of East Jerusalem and the entire Gaza Strip,[^cite_note-Karsh-218][^cite_note-219] as well as a stipulation that 69 Jewish settlements (which comprise 85% of the West Bank's Jewish settlers) would be ceded to Israel, no right of return to Israel, no sovereignty over the Temple Mount or any core East Jerusalem neighborhoods, and continued Israel control over the Jordan Valley.[^cite_note-220][^cite_note-nybooks.com-221]

Arafat rejected this offer,[^cite_note-Karsh-218] which Palestinian negotiators, Israeli analysts and Israeli Foreign Minister Shlomo Ben-Ami described as "unacceptable".[^cite_note-giim_norman-211][^cite_note-222] According to the Palestinian negotiators the offer did not remove many of the elements of the Israeli occupation regarding land, security, settlements, and Jerusalem.[^cite_note-JPressman-223]

After the Camp David summit, a narrative emerged, supported by Israeli Prime Minister Ehud Barak and his foreign minister Shlomo Ben-Ami, as well as US officials including Dennis Ross and Madeleine Albright, that Yasser Arafat had rejected a generous peace offer from Israel and instead incited a violent uprising. This narrative suggested that Arafat was not interested in a two-state solution, but rather aimed to destroy Israel and take over all of Palestine. This view was widely accepted in US and Israeli public opinion. Nearly all scholars and most Israeli and US officials involved in the negotiations have rejected this narrative. These individuals include prominent Israeli negotiators, the IDF chief of staff, the head of the IDF's intelligence bureau, the head of the Shin Bet as well as their advisors.[^cite_note-Jerome_Slater-224]

No tenable solution was crafted which would satisfy both Israeli and Palestinian demands, even under intense US pressure. Clinton has long blamed Arafat for the collapse of the summit.[^cite_note-225] In the months following the summit, Clinton appointed former US Senator [George J. Mitchell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_J._Mitchell) to lead a fact-finding committee aiming to identify strategies for restoring the peace process. The [committee's findings were published in 2001](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mitchell_Report_\(Arab%E2%80%93Israeli_conflict\)) with the dismantlement of existing Israeli settlements and Palestinian crackdown on militant activity being one strategy.[^cite_note-226]

### Developments following Camp David

Main article: [The Clinton Parameters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Clinton_Parameters)



[Israeli West Bank barrier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israeli_West_Bank_barrier) in [Bethlehem](4312.md)

Following the failed summit Palestinian and Israeli negotiators continued to meet in small groups through August and September 2000 to try to bridge the gaps between their respective positions. The United States prepared its own plan to resolve the outstanding issues. Clinton's presentation of the US proposals was delayed by the advent of the [Second Intifada](71717.md) at the end of September.[^cite_note-JPressman-223]

Clinton's plan, eventually presented on 23 December 2000, proposed the establishment of a sovereign Palestinian state in the Gaza Strip and 94–96 percent of the West Bank plus the equivalent of 1–3 percent of the West Bank in land swaps from pre-1967 Israel. On Jerusalem, the plan stated that "the general principle is that Arab areas are Palestinian and that Jewish areas are Israeli." The holy sites were to be split on the basis that Palestinians would have sovereignty over the Temple Mount/Noble sanctuary, while the Israelis would have sovereignty over the Western Wall. On refugees, the plan suggested a number of proposals including financial compensation, the right of return to the Palestinian state, and Israeli acknowledgment of suffering caused to the Palestinians in 1948. Security proposals referred to a "non-militarized" Palestinian state, and an international force for border security. Both sides accepted Clinton's plan[^cite_note-JPressman-223][^cite_note-USEmbassy-227][^cite_note-NYTsCP-228] and it became the basis for the negotiations at the Taba Peace summit the following January.[^cite_note-JPressman-223]

### Taba Summit (2001)

Main article: [Taba Summit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taba_Summit)

The Israeli negotiation team presented a new map at the [Taba Summit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taba_Summit) in [Taba, Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taba_\(Egypt\)), in January 2001. The proposition removed the "temporarily Israeli controlled" areas, and the Palestinian side accepted this as a basis for further negotiation. With Israeli elections looming the talks ended without an agreement but the two sides issued a joint statement attesting to the progress they had made: "The sides declare that they have never been closer to reaching an agreement and it is thus our shared belief that the remaining gaps could be bridged with the resumption of negotiations following the Israeli elections." The following month the [Likud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Likud) party candidate [Ariel Sharon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ariel_Sharon) defeated Ehud Barak in the Israeli elections and was elected as Israeli prime minister on 7 February 2001. Sharon's new government chose not to resume the high-level talks.[^cite_note-JPressman-223]

### Road map for peace (2002–2003)

Main article: [Road map for peace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Road_map_for_peace)



President [George W. Bush](3414021.md), center, discusses the peace process with Prime Minister [Ariel Sharon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ariel_Sharon) of Israel, left, and Palestinian President [Mahmoud Abbas](232595.md) in [Aqaba](328399.md), Jordan, 4 June 2003.

One peace proposal, presented by the [Quartet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quartet_on_the_Middle_East) of the European Union, Russia, the United Nations and the United States on 17 September 2002, was the Road Map for Peace. This plan did not attempt to resolve difficult questions such as the fate of Jerusalem or Israeli settlements, but left that to be negotiated in later phases of the process. The proposal never made it beyond the first phase, whose goals called for a halt to both Israeli settlement construction and Israeli–Palestinian violence. Neither goal has been achieved as of November 2015.[^cite_note-229][^cite_note-230][^cite_note-231]



The Israeli proposal of the exchange of territories at the Annapolis conference, according to the Economic Cooperation Foundation think-tank (blue to Israel, green to the Palestinian state)

The Annapolis Conference was a Middle East [peace conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peace_conference) held on 27 November 2007, at the [United States Naval Academy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Naval_Academy) in [Annapolis, Maryland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Annapolis,_Maryland), United States. The conference aimed to revive the [Israeli–Palestinian peace process](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peace_process_in_the_Israeli%E2%80%93Palestinian_conflict) and implement the *"[Roadmap for peace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roadmap_for_peace)"*.[^cite_note-232] The conference ended with the issuing of a joint statement from all parties. After the Annapolis Conference, the negotiations were continued. Both Mahmoud Abbas and Ehud Olmert presented each other with competing peace proposals. Ultimately no agreement was reached.[^cite_note-Joint_Understanding-233][^cite_note-234]

### Arab Peace Initiative (2002, 2007, 2017)

Main article: [Arab Peace Initiative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Peace_Initiative)



[U.S. Secretary of State John Kerry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Kerry) meets with an Arab Peace Initiative delegation in Paris, 2013.

The Arab Peace Initiative ([Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_language): مبادرة السلام العربية *Mubādirat as-Salām al-ʿArabīyyah*), also known as the Saudi Initiative, was first proposed by Crown Prince [Abdullah of Saudi Arabia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdullah_of_Saudi_Arabia) at the 2002 [Beirut summit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2002_Arab_League_summit). The initiative is a proposed solution to the Arab–Israeli conflict as a whole, and the Israeli–Palestinian conflict in particular.[^cite_note-235] The initiative was initially published on 28 March 2002, at the Beirut summit, and agreed upon again at the 2007 [Riyadh summit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2007_Arab_League_summit). Unlike the Road Map for Peace, it spelled out "final solution" borders based on the UN borders established before the 1967 [Six-Day War](29328.md). It offered full normalization of relations with Israel, in exchange for the withdrawal of its forces from all the occupied territories, including the [Golan Heights](7515865.md), to recognize "an independent Palestinian state with East Jerusalem as its capital" in the West Bank and Gaza Strip, as well as a "just solution" for the Palestinian refugees.[^cite_note-236]

The [Palestinian Authority](24093.md) led by [Yasser Arafat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yasser_Arafat) immediately embraced the initiative.[^cite_note-237] His successor [Mahmoud Abbas](232595.md) also supported the plan and officially asked U.S. President [Barack Obama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barack_Obama) to adopt it as part of his Middle East policy.[^cite_note-Haaretz1-238] [Islamist](15012.md) political party [Hamas](13913.md), [the elected government](38945340.md) of the [Gaza Strip](12047.md), was deeply divided,[^cite_note-al-zahar-239] with most factions rejecting the plan.[^cite_note-Time-240] Palestinians have criticized the [Israel–United Arab Emirates normalization agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israel%E2%80%93United_Arab_Emirates_normalization_agreement) and [another with Bahrain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bahrain%E2%80%93Israel_normalization_agreement) signed in September 2020, fearing the moves weaken the Arab Peace Initiative, regarding the UAE's move as "a betrayal."[^cite_note-jpost061020-241]

The Israeli government under [Ariel Sharon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ariel_Sharon) rejected the initiative as a "non-starter"[^cite_note-bbc-242] because it required Israel to withdraw to pre-June 1967 borders.[^cite_note-243] After the renewed Arab League endorsement in 2007, then-Prime Minister [Ehud Olmert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ehud_Olmert) gave a cautious welcome to the plan.[^cite_note-244] In 2015, Israeli Prime Minister [Benjamin Netanyahu](56472.md) expressed tentative support for the Initiative,[^cite_note-Times_of_Israel-245] but in 2018, he rejected it as a basis for future negotiations with the Palestinians.[^cite_note-haaretz2018apr10-246]

## Current status

### Apartheid

Main article: [Israeli apartheid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israeli_apartheid)

In April 2021, [Human Rights Watch](46530.md) released its report *A Threshold Crossed*, describing the [policies of Israel towards Palestinians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israeli_apartheid) living in Israel, the West Bank and Gaza constituted the [crime of apartheid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crime_of_apartheid).[^cite_note-Holmes-247] As of 2022, various Israeli and international human rights organizations were in agreement with the report Israeli actions constituted the [crime of apartheid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crime_of_apartheid).[^cite_note-israpa-54] A further report titled *Israel's Apartheid Against Palestinians: Cruel System of Domination and Crime Against Humanity* was released by [Amnesty International](18947898.md) on 1 February 2022.[^cite_note-248] However, others have disagreed with the characterization. The head of the [International Committee of the Red Cross](5023832.md) to Israel has said the organization rejects the charge.[^cite_note-249] In 2023, US House of Representatives passed a resolution calling the Apartheid claim "absurd."[^cite_note-250]

In 2018, the [Knesset](74656.md) passed the [Nation-State law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basic_Law:_Israel_as_the_Nation-State_of_the_Jewish_People) which the Israeli legal NGO [Adalah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adalah_\(legal_center\)) nicknamed the "Apartheid law." Adalah described the Nation-State law as "constitutionally enshrining Jewish supremacy and the identity of the State of Israel as the nation-state of the Jewish people." The Nation-State law is a [Basic Law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basic_Laws_of_Israel), meaning that it has "quasi-constitutional status,"[^cite_note-251] and states that the right to exercise national self-determination in Israel is "unique to the Jewish people".[^cite_note-252] In July 2024, the [International Court of Justice](14918.md) determined that Israeli policies violate the [International Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Racial Discrimination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Convention_on_the_Elimination_of_All_Forms_of_Racial_Discrimination).[^cite_note-Haaretz-253] Law Professor Solon Solomon said "the court's stance suggests a hesitation to explicitly opine" on the apartheid claim.[^cite_note-254]

### Occupied Palestinian territory

See also: [Israeli-occupied territories](2781576.md), [West Bank § Status](33209.md#Status), [Positions on Jerusalem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Positions_on_Jerusalem), and [Status of territories captured by Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Status_of_territories_captured_by_Israel)



Protest against land confiscation held at [Bil'in](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bil'in), 2011

Israel has occupied the [Palestinian territories](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_territories), which comprise the [West Bank](33209.md) (including [East Jerusalem](343508.md)) and the [Gaza Strip](12047.md), since the 1967 Six-Day War, making it the longest military occupation in modern history.[^cite_note-AIA-255] In 2024, the [International Court of Justice](14918.md) determined that the Palestinian territories constitute one political unit and that Israel's occupation since 1967, and the subsequent creation of [Israeli settlements](15123.md) and exploitation of natural resources, are illegal under international law. The court also ruled that Israel should pay full [reparations](29967971.md) to the [Palestinian people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_people) for the damage the occupation has caused.[^cite_note-256][^cite_note-257]

Some Palestinians say they are entitled to all of the West Bank, Gaza Strip, and East Jerusalem. Israel says it is justified in not ceding all this land, because of security concerns, and also because the lack of any valid diplomatic agreement at the time means that ownership and boundaries of this land is open for discussion.[^cite_note-Eran-258] Palestinians believe any reduction of their territorial claims is a severe deprivation of their rights. In negotiations, the Palestinian stance is that any moves to reduce the boundaries of this land is a hostile move against their key interests. Israel considers this land to be in dispute and feels the purpose of negotiations is to define what the final borders will be. In 2017 Hamas announced that it was ready to support a Palestinian state on the 1967 borders "without recognising Israel or ceding any rights".[^cite_note-259]

#### Israeli settlements

Main article: [Israeli settlement](15123.md)



A neighborhood in the settlement of [Ariel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ariel_\(city\)) in the [Israeli occupied](2781576.md) [West Bank](33209.md), which is home to the [Ariel University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ariel_University)



Israeli settlers in [Hebron](38577.md), West Bank

The international community considers [Israeli settlements to be illegal under international law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_law_and_Israeli_settlements),[^cite_note-260][^cite_note-maj-261][^cite_note-262][^cite_note-263] but Israel disputes this.[^cite_note-264][^cite_note-Harel-265][^cite_note-Stone2004-266][^cite_note-Byron2013-267] Those who justify the legality of the settlements use arguments based upon [Articles 2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_law_and_Israeli_settlements#Article_2) and [49](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_law_and_Israeli_settlements#Article_49) of the Fourth [Geneva Convention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geneva_Convention), as well as [UN Security Council Resolution 242](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UN_Security_Council_Resolution_242).[^cite_note-268] The expansion of settlements often involves the confiscation of Palestinian land and resources, leading to displacement of Palestinian communities and creating a source of tension and conflict. Settlements are often protected by the Israeli military and are frequently flashpoints for violence against Palestinians.
